
**Corrections made during implementation** (this task's own first review
pass — the plan's original snippets returned `dbq.Project` directly, had
no slug validation, no membership-management methods, and no error
mapping for a not-found project):

1. `Create`, `ListForUser`, `BySlug` and the new `ByID`/`ListMembers` now
   return the domain types `Project` and `Member`, never `dbq.Project` or
   the generated `ListMembersRow`. The original snippet returned
   `dbq.Project` directly from every public method — exactly the thing
   `identity.User`'s own doc comment (Task 5) explains why the identity
   package never does: a raw sqlc struct is one careless `writeJSON` away
   from serving whatever the generated type happens to carry, and here it
   would also have forced every caller outside this package to import
   `dbq` and unwrap `pgtype.Timestamptz` just to read a project's slug.
2. Slugs are now validated and normalised. The original plan inserted
   whatever string was handed to `Create` with no shape check at all,
   despite the spec's own `/g/<slug>/...` URL scheme depending on the
   slug being a safe path segment — a slug containing a slash, whitespace,
   or nothing at all would have produced a project nothing could link to
   correctly, or (worse) one whose slug could be crafted to break the
   route. `Create` now lower-cases and trims the slug (matching
   `GetProjectBySlug`'s own case-insensitive comparison, so the stored
   value is always exactly what a working URL needs) and rejects anything
   outside `^[a-z0-9]+(-[a-z0-9]+)*$` within 1-64 runes as
   `ErrSlugInvalid`. `TestDuplicateSlugRejected`'s `"AZEROTH"` case still
   works exactly as originally written: it normalises to `azeroth`, which
   collides with the first project's slug, so it still fails on
   `ErrSlugTaken` rather than the newly added `ErrSlugInvalid`. `name`
   gained the same trim-and-bound treatment (1-200 runes, `ErrNameInvalid`)
   as `identity.prepareUser` already applies to `DisplayName`.
3. `BySlug` (and the new `ByID`) now return a package-level
   `ErrProjectNotFound` instead of leaking `pgx.ErrNoRows` — the original
   snippet's `return dbq.Project{}, pgx.ErrNoRows` handed every caller a
   `pgx`-specific sentinel to depend on, which is exactly the kind of
   leak `identity.Service` never allows (`UserForSession` maps the same
   case to `ErrNoSession`, `RoleOf` already mapped it to `ErrNotAMember`).
4. Added `SetRole` and `RemoveMember`, and the `CountOwnersForUpdate`
   query and `ErrLastOwner` they both depend on. The original plan defined
   `DeleteMembership` and `ListMembers` as queries but never wrote a
   service method for either, and had no way at all to change an existing
   member's role or to add a second member to a project short of a second
   call to `Create` (which would attempt to insert a second row into a
   table with no such capability, and isn't how a real invite-driven
   membership grant works anyway — see `identity.RedeemInvite`, which
   already calls `UpsertMembership` directly). Left as originally
   specified, this task shipped a way to create a project and read
   memberships back, but no way to ever add, promote, demote or remove a
   member — and, worse, nothing stopped a project's sole owner from being
   demoted or removed, human error or a compromised session either one,
   leaving every row that hangs off that project (every future entity,
   token, and membership) permanently unmanageable. `SetRole` now handles
   both "add a member" and "change an existing member's role" (matching
   `UpsertMembership`'s own insert-or-update semantics, so there is one
   method rather than two duplicating the same guard), and both `SetRole`
   and `RemoveMember` refuse a change that would leave the project with no
   owner. The guard is race-safe: `CountOwnersForUpdate` takes a
   `FOR UPDATE` row lock on every owner membership of the project before
   counting, inside the same transaction as the demotion or removal, so
   two concurrent attempts to remove a project's last two owners serialize
   — the first to commit succeeds, and the second re-evaluates against the
   now-updated state and correctly fails with `ErrLastOwner` instead of
   both succeeding and leaving zero owners. This mirrors the row-locking
   argument `RedeemInvite`'s own doc comment (Task 7) makes for why a
   conditional `UPDATE`'s row lock is what actually prevents a concurrent
   double-redemption, not the earlier read. `TestConcurrentRemovalLeaves
   ExactlyOneOwner` (Step 4's test file) exercises this with two
   goroutines across five runs — see Correction 9 below for why that test
   was added in a second pass rather than this first one.
5. `RoleOf` deliberately still does not distinguish "no such project" from
   "this project exists but you're not a member" — both remain
   `ErrNotAMember`. This was double-checked, not changed: `RoleOf` is an
   authorization check, and telling a non-member which project IDs
   correspond to real (if inaccessible) projects would leak exactly the
   kind of cross-game information the spec's isolation invariant ("a
   token touching another game gets `scope_violation`, admin or not")
   exists to prevent. A caller that needs the two told apart for a normal
   lookup — a 404 page for an unknown slug, say — uses `ByID`/
   `BySlugForUser`, which exist precisely because they *do* distinguish
   "not found" from anything else while still carrying no membership
   information to a non-member (see Correction 12 below for why the slug
   half of that statement needed a second pass of its own).
6. No instance-admin bypass was added anywhere in this package, and none
   is planned for it. The spec is explicit that game isolation applies
   "admin or not" and that even an admin's bearer token is scoped to one
   project; `ListForUser` and `RoleOf` both stay membership-only for every
   caller regardless of `users.is_admin`. An admin-facing cross-project
   view (if the product ever wants one) is a deliberately separate,
   explicitly-admin-only capability for a later task to add on purpose —
   not something this task should back into by quietly special-casing an
   `IsAdmin` flag inside the same methods every non-admin caller also
   calls.
7. Test file grew from the plan's four tests to sixteen, covering every
   correction above: slug normalisation and shape validation, `BySlug`/
   `ByID` not-found behaviour, `ListMembers`, `SetRole` (promotion,
   invalid role, demoting a sole owner), `RemoveMember` (sole owner,
   second owner present, no-op on a non-member), and `RoleOf` against an
   unknown project ID specifically (Correction 5).
8. `SetRole`, `RemoveMember`, `ListMembers`, `CountOwnersForUpdate` and
   `ErrLastOwner` have no caller anywhere in the plan before Task 12: a
   quality review flagged this correctly as a real gap, not a false
   positive, but recommended deleting the methods rather than giving them
   a consumer. They stay — they are correct, tested, and guard something
   real the moment membership management exists — and Task 12 has been
   amended instead: it now specifies `GET/PATCH/DELETE
   /api/games/{game}/members[/{user}]`, the authorization rule for each
   (only an owner may change a role; anyone may remove themselves; a
   token caller may manage no membership at all), and the `409
   last_owner` mapping for `ErrLastOwner`. If a future reader reaches this
   point and Task 12 is still unimplemented, that amendment is where the
   consumer is specified — do not repeat this review question.

**Round 2 corrections** (a second quality review of the committed Task 8,
after Task 12's amendment above; the review praised the
`CountOwnersForUpdate` concurrency comment specifically and confirmed the
package reads as a genuine sibling of `identity`, then found one critical
hole and a set of things that get harder once Task 12 builds handlers on
this):

9. **(Critical.)** The last-owner invariant had a back door Correction 4's
   Go-only guard could never close: `memberships.user_id` is
   `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so deleting a user deletes their memberships
   underneath this package entirely, skipping `SetRole` and
   `RemoveMember` — and therefore their `CountOwnersForUpdate` lock —
   completely. Nothing deletes users today (no such endpoint exists
   anywhere in this plan yet), so this was latent rather than active, but
   it would have gone live silently the day one landed, with any orphaned
   projects it created already committed by the time anyone noticed.
   Closed at the one layer the cascade cannot bypass: Step 2 above adds
   migration 0002, a deferred constraint trigger on `memberships` that
   asserts a project keeps at least one owner after any `DELETE` or
   `UPDATE` of an owner row, with an explicit escape hatch
   (`NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM projects WHERE id = OLD.project_id)`) for
   the case where the project itself is mid-deletion and the cascade is
   supposed to remove that last owner row along with everything else.
   `TestDeletingSoleOwnerUserIsBlockedByDatabase`,
   `TestDeletingNonSoleOwnerUserSucceeds` and
   `TestDeletingProjectCascadesDespiteTrigger` (Step 4's test file) issue
   the `DELETE` directly against `users`/`projects` rather than through
   any Go method, precisely to prove the database trigger — not this
   package's Go code — is what stops it.

   The related product dead end this guard creates, on purpose but worth
   naming: with the guard in place and no `DeleteProject` anywhere in this
   plan, a designer who creates a game alone can never leave it or delete
   it — the only exit today is an operator in `psql`. Not building that
   now, deliberately (Task 8 is the domain layer; deletion needs the same
   authorization thinking Task 12 already had to do for role changes and
   removal) — but it is now a named Task 12 item, not a silent gap: see
   Task 12's own file list, which now includes
   `DELETE /api/games/{game}` (owner-only, mapping `ErrLastOwner` the
   same way role-change and removal already do) as a follow-on to its
   membership endpoints, with a note that it is what makes this guard
   survivable rather than a trap.
10. `SetRole` against an unknown project or user, and `Create` with an
    unknown creator, used to surface as a raw `SQLSTATE 23503` — the same
    class of defect `ErrSlugTaken`'s constraint-name mapping (Correction
    2/3) already existed to avoid, just for `23503`
    (foreign_key_violation) instead of `23505` (unique_violation).
    `mapMembershipInsertError` now maps `memberships_project_id_fkey` to
    `ErrProjectNotFound` and the new `memberships_user_id_fkey` to the new
    `ErrUserNotFound`, and both `Create`'s and `SetRole`'s
    `UpsertMembership` call sites route through it before falling back to
    a generic wrapped error. Without this, Task 12's handlers would have
    returned 500 for what are actually 404s.
11. `Member` no longer carries `Email`. `ListMembers` is
    authorization-free by design (Correction 4/8), so every caller
    holding a `Member` held everyone's email — including a viewer with no
    business reading a teammate's address. Display name, id and role are
    what a member list renders; `ListMembers`'s underlying query no
    longer selects `u.email` at all, not just at the Go layer, so an
    owner-only contact-details view, if the product ever wants one, has
    to be a query someone adds on purpose rather than a field this one
    already happened to be carrying.
12. `BySlug` was an enumeration oracle: slugs are human-chosen game names,
    so a raw slug lookup let any authenticated user probe whether
    `azeroth` exists — exactly the cross-game inference `RoleOf` (and
    ErrNotAMember's own doc comment, Correction 5) goes out of its way to
    prevent, and the method's own doc comment at the time invited exactly
    that. Renamed to the unexported `bySlug` and paired with the new
    `BySlugForUser(ctx, slug, userID)`, which resolves the slug and checks
    membership in the same call, returning `ErrProjectNotFound` for both
    "no such slug" and "a real game you're just not in" — the same
    non-distinction `RoleOf` already makes for ids, now made for slugs
    too. `bySlug` has no caller outside this package and none is
    currently planned before a `/g/{slug}` route exists to need it (see
    Task 15, where `/g/{game}` today only serves a static SPA shell with
    no server-side slug resolution at all); the moment one is added, it
    must call `BySlugForUser`, never the unexported form.
13. `ORDER BY p.name` and `ORDER BY u.display_name` ordered by non-unique
    columns, so two games (or two members) sharing a name could reshuffle
    between calls. Both queries now add `, id` as a tiebreak (`ORDER BY
    p.name, p.id` and `ORDER BY u.display_name, u.id`). Both stay
    unpaginated — right for this product's scale — but a stable order is
    what makes adding keyset pagination later a non-event instead of a
    behaviour change. `TestListForUserOrderingIsStableOnTies` and
    `TestListMembersOrderingIsStableOnTies` (Step 4's test file) are the
    tests that would have caught Correction 7/13 shipping broken: every
    other test in the file uses a distinct slug or a display name derived
    from a unique email, which is exactly how a same-name tie went
    untested through the first review pass.
14. The role set lived in four places at once even after Correction 4 —
    two CHECK constraints, `identity.inviteRoles`, and this package's own
    `membershipRoles` — plus bare `"owner"` string literals at three call
    sites. A fourth role would have meant a migration and two separate Go
    edits, with nothing failing at compile time if one was missed. Step 1
    above adds `internal/roles`, a `Role` type with the three constants
    and a `Valid` validator that both `identity` (Step 7) and `projects`
    import instead of declaring their own copy; `projects.go` keeps a
    single `ownerRole = string(roles.Owner)` constant rather than
    threading the `Role` type through every signature, since every role
    value in this package still arrives as a plain string from the
    database or, eventually, an HTTP body. `TestAllRolesAcceptedByDatabase`
    is the test that actually catches drift between this package and
    migration 0001's CHECK constraint: it walks `roles.All()`, inserts
    each one directly (bypassing `SetRole`'s own Go-side validation) and
    asserts the database accepts it, then asserts a bogus role is
    rejected — a doc comment asserting the two stay in sync cannot catch
    the day they don't; this test can.
15. Reserved and UUID-shaped slugs are now rejected. `new`, `api`,
    `static`, `login` and `healthz` were all accepted, and so was a
    literal UUID string — harmless today only because `/g/` has no
    sibling routes yet and the API is UUID-addressed, not slug-addressed.
    `validateSlug` now rejects any slug in a `reservedSlugs` set (built
    from every top-level path segment this plan's routes already claim,
    plus `new` for a future create-game page) and anything `uuid.Parse`
    accepts, so a slug can never later collide with a route or be mistaken
    for an id by a handler that accepts either.
16. `validateName` now rejects control runes (`unicode.IsControl`) and the
    Unicode bidirectional-override characters (`U+202A`-`U+202E`,
    `U+2066`-`U+2069`) — a newline or a bidi override previously stored
    verbatim, and names are rendered into page titles, the game picker and
    SSE payloads, none of which expected either. `TestCreateRejectsInvalidName`
    (previously absent — `ErrNameInvalid` was defined in the first pass
    but never actually asserted by a test) now covers this alongside the
    empty and too-long cases.
17. `bySlug` (Correction 12) now runs its input through `validateSlug`
    before querying, instead of handing an unvalidated, unbounded URL
    segment straight to `GetProjectBySlug`. This was a gap specific to
    the slug-lookup path — `Create` always validated its slug argument,
    but a lookup by slug never did.
18. `internal/db/migrate_internal_test.go`'s `TestMigrateUpDownUp` needed
    a second `migrateDown` call once migration 0002 existed: the test
    asserted `users` was gone after a single rollback, true when 0001 was
    the only migration, false now that rolling back "the most recent
    migration" only undoes 0002. See Step 8 above.
19. `withTx` stays duplicated between `identity.Service` and
    `projects.Service` rather than being extracted to a shared
    `internal/db` helper. Both this task's own review and the later
    quality review agreed: two copies of four lines is fine, three would
    not be, and the extraction is clean and available the moment the
    metamodel service (a third consumer) arrives — no need to do it
    speculatively now.

**Round 3 correction** (a re-review of Round 2's own fix, approving it in
full and finding exactly one thing wrong — not with the code, with what a
comment claimed about it):

20. `TestConcurrentRemovalLeavesExactlyOneOwner`'s doc comment, and the
    `CountOwnersForUpdate`/`SetRole`/`RemoveMember` comments pointing at
    it, said the test was "the test `FOR UPDATE` exists for" and that
    dropping the row lock would leave it silently green — Correction 9's
    own framing, written before migration 0002's trigger existed to
    check against. The reviewer stripped `FOR UPDATE` from
    `CountOwnersForUpdate` and ran the test roughly eighty times: it
    never failed, because the trigger's deferred, commit-time check
    already serializes the same two-goroutine race independently of any
    application-level lock — under READ COMMITTED, only one of two
    concurrent transactions can be the second to reach that check before
    commit, so the trigger alone catches the race the lock was credited
    with catching. This project has spent eight tasks removing comments
    that overclaimed what they guarded (the sweep that "prevented" memory
    exhaustion when it bounded it; the guard that read as tidiness when
    it was an authentication bypass), and a comment asserting a test
    proves a lock load-bearing is exactly how someone later deletes that
    lock, watches the suite stay green, and ships it. All four comments
    (the query's, both call sites', and the test's) now say what is
    actually true: the invariant is defended in two independent layers,
    dropping either one alone does not turn this test red, and the lock's
    remaining, real value is failing fast inside this package with a
    typed `ErrLastOwner` instead of the transaction aborting on a raw
    trigger exception from a lock-free path. No code, query, or test
    changed — comments only, verified against an unchanged `sqlc diff`
    and an unchanged, still-passing test suite.

### Task 9: API tokens bound to one project

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/identity/tokens.go`, `internal/db/migrations/0003_api_token_hint.sql`
- Modify: `internal/db/queries/identity.sql`, `internal/db/queries/projects.sql`,
  `internal/projects/projects.go`, `internal/db/migrate_internal_test.go`
- Test: `internal/identity/tokens_test.go`, `internal/projects/projects_test.go`

(The shape below is the final, post-round-2 shape — see the Round 2
corrections at the end of this task for what a second review changed and
why: closing the membership-removal hole in this task instead of Task 12,
the token checksum and hint, the throttle pre-check, and several smaller
fixes.)

- [ ] **Step 1: Add the token-hint column**

`internal/db/migrations/0003_api_token_hint.sql` — a new migration, not an
edit to the applied 0001, per this codebase's rule against touching a
migration once it has shipped:

```sql
-- api_tokens.token_hint stores a short, non-secret discriminator derived
-- from the clear token's random body (never from its hash, and never a
-- prefix long enough to matter for guessing): enough for an operator who
-- finds "mst_xK9…" in a leaked log or a committed .env to find the
-- matching row via ListAPITokens without being able to reconstruct the
-- token, or anything close to it, from the hint alone.
-- +goose Up
ALTER TABLE api_tokens ADD COLUMN token_hint text NOT NULL DEFAULT '';
ALTER TABLE api_tokens ALTER COLUMN token_hint DROP DEFAULT;

-- +goose Down
ALTER TABLE api_tokens DROP COLUMN token_hint;
```

Update `TestMigrateUpDownUp` (`internal/db/migrate_internal_test.go`) for
a third migration: a third `migrateDown` call, rolling back 0003 before
0002 and 0001.

- [ ] **Step 2: Add the identity queries**

Append to `internal/db/queries/identity.sql`:

```sql
-- name: CreateAPIToken :one
INSERT INTO api_tokens (token_hash, project_id, user_id, label, token_hint)
VALUES (sqlc.arg('token_hash')::bytea, sqlc.arg('project_id')::uuid,
        sqlc.arg('user_id')::uuid, sqlc.arg('label')::text, sqlc.arg('token_hint')::text)
RETURNING *;

-- name: GetLiveAPIToken :one
SELECT * FROM api_tokens
WHERE token_hash = sqlc.arg('token_hash')::bytea AND revoked_at IS NULL;

-- name: TouchAPIToken :exec
-- Throttled: this runs on every authenticated agent request, so an
-- unconditional UPDATE would take a row lock on the hot path for no
-- observable benefit. Only write when the existing timestamp is missing or
-- more than five minutes stale. The five-minute interval here must match
-- identity.touchThrottle in tokens.go, which pre-checks the same
-- condition in Go before ever issuing this statement (so the common case
-- skips the round trip entirely, not just the lock) — this WHERE clause
-- stays authoritative regardless, since it is what actually makes the
-- write safe under two concurrent resolves of the same token, which the
-- Go pre-check alone cannot guarantee. Change one, change both.
UPDATE api_tokens SET last_used_at = now()
WHERE id = sqlc.arg('id')::uuid
  AND (last_used_at IS NULL OR last_used_at < now() - interval '5 minutes');

-- name: RevokeAPIToken :exec
-- Scoped to project_id as well as id: revoking an id that exists but
-- belongs to a different project, like revoking an unknown or
-- already-revoked id, is a no-op rather than an error — the caller's goal
-- (no live token under this id in this project) is already satisfied, the
-- same convention RevokeSession and RevokeInvite already establish. This
-- also means the project scope of the caller is enforced by the query
-- itself, not by a separate ownership check the caller could forget.
UPDATE api_tokens SET revoked_at = now()
WHERE id = sqlc.arg('id')::uuid AND project_id = sqlc.arg('project_id')::uuid
  AND revoked_at IS NULL;

-- name: ListAPITokens :many
-- token_hash is deliberately not selected: this query backs an operator
-- listing (ListAPITokens in tokens.go), and the hash of a bearer
-- credential has no reason to leave the database even in a column nothing
-- currently renders. token_hint is selected instead — see its column
-- comment (migration 0003) for what it is safe to show. Revoked rows are
-- included on purpose: this is the audit trail for what happened to a
-- token, not only a list of what is still live. Joined to users so an
-- operator triaging a leaked token sees who minted it instead of a raw
-- id, and ordered by created_at then id so ties (two tokens minted in
-- the same transaction) list in a stable order across repeated calls,
-- the same tiebreak reasoning ListProjectsForUser and ListMembers
-- already apply to their own orderings.
SELECT t.id, t.project_id, t.user_id, u.display_name AS user_display_name,
       t.label, t.token_hint, t.created_at, t.last_used_at, t.revoked_at
FROM api_tokens t
JOIN users u ON u.id = t.user_id
WHERE t.project_id = sqlc.arg('project_id')::uuid
ORDER BY t.created_at DESC, t.id DESC;
```

Also fix `ListOutstandingInvites`'s ordering in the same file, which
shares the missing-tiebreak drift ListAPITokens above just closed:

```sql
-- name: ListOutstandingInvites :many
-- "Outstanding" means not yet redeemed, regardless of whether it has since
-- expired: an admin looking for a mis-sent invite to revoke needs to find
-- it before it necessarily expires on its own, and a lapsed-but-unredeemed
-- row is also useful context ("this one needs reissuing"). Ordered
-- newest-first, with id as an explicit tiebreak (matching ListAPITokens
-- and every other listing in this file that learned this lesson first) so
-- two invites created in the same transaction don't reshuffle between
-- calls.
SELECT * FROM invites WHERE redeemed_at IS NULL ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC;
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Add the membership-scoped revocation query and wire it into `RemoveMember`**

Append to `internal/db/queries/projects.sql`:

```sql
-- name: RevokeAPITokensForMember :exec
-- Called from RemoveMember, inside the same transaction as the
-- membership deletion: every live API token in this project created by
-- the removed member is revoked — not every token that member has ever
-- created across every project they belong to, only the ones scoped to
-- the project they just lost access to. This is SQL over api_tokens, not
-- a reason for this package to import identity: the query runs through
-- the same dbq.Queries handle UpsertMembership above already crosses in
-- the other direction (UpsertMembership is defined in identity.sql and
-- called from this package). Idempotent and already project-scoped for
-- the same reason identity's own RevokeAPIToken is (see that query's
-- comment): a member with no tokens, or already-revoked ones, is a
-- harmless no-op, so RemoveMember never needs to check what exists
-- before calling this.
UPDATE api_tokens SET revoked_at = now()
WHERE user_id = sqlc.arg('user_id')::uuid AND project_id = sqlc.arg('project_id')::uuid
  AND revoked_at IS NULL;
```

Then change `RemoveMember` in `internal/projects/projects.go` (Task 8) to
call it inside its existing transaction, after the membership row is
gone:

```go
func (s *Service) RemoveMember(ctx context.Context, userID, projectID uuid.UUID) error {
	return s.withTx(ctx, func(q *dbq.Queries) error {
		current, err := q.GetMembershipRole(ctx, dbq.GetMembershipRoleParams{UserID: userID, ProjectID: projectID})
		if err != nil {
			if errors.Is(err, pgx.ErrNoRows) {
				return nil
			}
			return fmt.Errorf("lookup membership: %w", err)
		}
		if current == ownerRole {
			n, cerr := q.CountOwnersForUpdate(ctx, projectID)
			if cerr != nil {
				return fmt.Errorf("count owners: %w", cerr)
			}
			if n <= 1 {
				return ErrLastOwner
			}
		}
		if err := q.DeleteMembership(ctx, dbq.DeleteMembershipParams{UserID: userID, ProjectID: projectID}); err != nil {
			return fmt.Errorf("remove member: %w", err)
		}
		if err := q.RevokeAPITokensForMember(ctx, dbq.RevokeAPITokensForMemberParams{UserID: userID, ProjectID: projectID}); err != nil {
			return fmt.Errorf("revoke member's tokens: %w", err)
		}
		return nil
	})
}
```

`RemoveMember`'s own doc comment gains a paragraph explaining this: it
revokes the removed member's tokens for this project only, in the same
transaction, and why that call belongs here rather than in `identity` or
in a future HTTP handler (Task 12) — see Round 2 Correction 9 below for
the fuller reasoning and for why this could not wait for Task 12.

Run: `make sqlc`

- [ ] **Step 4: Write the failing test**

`internal/identity/tokens_test.go`. This is a representative subset —
the real file has grown to twelve tests across two rounds of review, plus
two more in `internal/projects/projects_test.go` covering the
membership-removal revocation Step 3 added — fourteen in total; see Round
2 Correction 12 below for the full list and why each was added.

```go
package identity_test

import (
	"context"
	"errors"
	"strings"
	"testing"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/identity"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/projects"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/testutil"
)

func TestAPITokenResolvesToItsProject(t *testing.T) {
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	ids := identity.New(pool, testConfig())
	projSvc := projects.New(pool)
	ctx := context.Background()

	user, _ := ids.CreateUser(ctx, identity.CreateUserRequest{Email: "designer@studio.com", DisplayName: "Designer", Password: "password12345"})
	project, _ := projSvc.Create(ctx, "azeroth", "Azeroth", user.ID)

	token, row, err := ids.CreateAPIToken(ctx, identity.CreateAPITokenRequest{
		ProjectID: project.ID,
		UserID:    user.ID,
		Label:     "seed agent",
	})
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("CreateAPIToken: %v", err)
	}
	if !strings.HasPrefix(token, identity.TokenPrefix) {
		t.Fatalf("token = %q, want the %s prefix", token, identity.TokenPrefix)
	}
	if row.Label != "seed agent" {
		t.Fatalf("label = %q", row.Label)
	}

	resolved, err := ids.ResolveAPIToken(ctx, token)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("ResolveAPIToken: %v", err)
	}
	if resolved.ProjectID != project.ID || resolved.UserID != user.ID {
		t.Fatal("the token resolved to the wrong project or user")
	}

	if err := ids.RevokeAPIToken(ctx, identity.RevokeAPITokenRequest{ProjectID: project.ID, TokenID: row.ID}); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("RevokeAPIToken: %v", err)
	}
	if _, err := ids.ResolveAPIToken(ctx, token); !errors.Is(err, identity.ErrTokenInvalid) {
		t.Fatalf("err = %v, want ErrTokenInvalid after revocation", err)
	}
}

func TestUnknownAPIToken(t *testing.T) {
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	ids := identity.New(pool, testConfig())
	if _, err := ids.ResolveAPIToken(context.Background(), "mst_nonsense"); !errors.Is(err, identity.ErrTokenInvalid) {
		t.Fatalf("err = %v, want ErrTokenInvalid", err)
	}
}
```

- [ ] **Step 6: Write the implementation**

`internal/identity/tokens.go`:

```go
package identity

import (
	"context"
	"crypto/sha256"
	"errors"
	"fmt"
	"hash/crc32"
	"log/slog"
	"strings"
	"time"
	"unicode/utf8"

	"github.com/google/uuid"
	"github.com/jackc/pgx/v5"
	"github.com/jackc/pgx/v5/pgconn"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/db/dbq"
)

// Bounds on an API token's label. It is free text an admin chooses to
// recognise a token in a list (ListAPITokens) — "seed agent",
// "nightly export" — not an identifier anything parses, so the same
// trim-and-rune-count treatment projects.validateName applies to a
// project's name applies here too.
const (
	minLabelRunes = 1
	maxLabelRunes = 200
)

// touchThrottle is how stale last_used_at must be before ResolveAPIToken
// bothers writing it again. It must match the "interval '5 minutes'"
// literal in TouchAPIToken's own SQL (identity.sql) — that WHERE clause,
// not this constant, is what actually makes the write safe under two
// concurrent resolves of the same token, since a plain Go time comparison
// has no way to serialize against a second goroutine doing the same
// check at the same moment. This constant exists so ResolveAPIToken can
// skip issuing the UPDATE at all in the overwhelmingly common case
// (already touched within the window), rather than sending a statement
// the database is guaranteed to match zero rows for on every single
// authenticated agent request.
const touchThrottle = 5 * time.Minute

// tokenChecksumRunes is the length, in base62 characters, of the checksum
// CreateAPIToken appends to a token's random body and ResolveAPIToken
// verifies before ever hashing or querying. crc32.ChecksumIEEE produces a
// 32-bit value; 62^6 exceeds 2^32, so six base62 characters always
// suffice with no risk of truncation.
const tokenChecksumRunes = 6

// tokenHintRunes is how much of a token's random body (before the
// checksum) CreateAPIToken stores, unencrypted, as TokenHint. It is
// deliberately short: enough for ListAPITokens to help an operator match
// a leaked value like "mst_xK9qP2af…" to the row it belongs to, nowhere
// near enough to reconstruct the token or meaningfully narrow a
// brute-force search of the remainder.
const tokenHintRunes = 8

// ErrTokenInvalid covers unknown and revoked bearer tokens, and a token
// whose checksum does not match its body (a mistyped or truncated
// paste — see verifyTokenChecksum). A revoked token is deliberately
// indistinguishable from one that never existed: GetLiveAPIToken's own
// WHERE clause excludes revoked rows from the lookup entirely, so
// ResolveAPIToken has no way to tell the two apart even if it wanted to,
// and it does not want to — the same reasoning ErrNoSession's doc comment
// (sessions.go) gives for collapsing an unknown token and an expired one
// into a single answer.
var ErrTokenInvalid = errors.New("api token is not valid")

// ErrTokenRequestInvalid means the request given to CreateAPIToken is
// malformed: an empty or over-long label, or a project or user id that
// does not exist (mapped from api_tokens' foreign key constraints, the
// same way projects.mapMembershipInsertError narrows a 23503 by
// constraint name).
var ErrTokenRequestInvalid = errors.New("token request is invalid")

// TokenPrefix marks a Maestro API token in logs and in the UI, and lets a
// human or a scanner recognise a leaked value on sight. It is inside the
// value ResolveAPIToken hashes (CreateAPIToken hashes TokenPrefix+body,
// not body alone), so changing it invalidates every token already
// issued — every stored hash was computed over the old prefix.
const TokenPrefix = "mst_"

// APITokenSummary is the identity domain's public view of an api_tokens
// row. It deliberately omits dbq.ApiToken's TokenHash, for the same
// reason User omits PasswordHash (users.go) and InviteSummary omits
// Invite's TokenHash (invites.go): nothing outside this package needs
// it, and nothing should be one careless writeJSON away from serving a
// value an attacker could brute-force offline against.
//
// LastUsedAt reflects the row as read at the start of ResolveAPIToken,
// before that call's own conditional touch (if any) is applied — so the
// value returned to whoever just made this exact call can be up to one
// touchThrottle interval stale relative to what the database holds
// immediately afterward. Callers that need the precise current value
// (an operator's listing, say) get it from ListAPITokens instead, which
// reads it fresh with no touch of its own.
//
// UserDisplayName is populated only by ListAPITokens, which joins users
// so an operator triaging a leaked token can see who minted it instead
// of a raw id; CreateAPIToken and ResolveAPIToken do not join users and
// always leave it empty.
type APITokenSummary struct {
	ID              uuid.UUID
	ProjectID       uuid.UUID
	UserID          uuid.UUID
	Label           string
	TokenHint       string
	UserDisplayName string
	CreatedAt       time.Time
	LastUsedAt      *time.Time
	RevokedAt       *time.Time
}

func apiTokenSummaryFrom(t dbq.ApiToken) APITokenSummary {
	out := APITokenSummary{
		ID:        t.ID,
		ProjectID: t.ProjectID,
		UserID:    t.UserID,
		Label:     t.Label,
		TokenHint: t.TokenHint,
		CreatedAt: t.CreatedAt.Time,
	}
	if t.LastUsedAt.Valid {
		lastUsed := t.LastUsedAt.Time
		out.LastUsedAt = &lastUsed
	}
	if t.RevokedAt.Valid {
		revoked := t.RevokedAt.Time
		out.RevokedAt = &revoked
	}
	return out
}

// CreateAPITokenRequest is the input to CreateAPIToken. It exists for the
// same reason CreateUserRequest (users.go) and InviteRequest (invites.go)
// do: ProjectID and UserID are both uuid.UUID, and a positional signature
// would let a transposed argument compile fine while silently minting a
// token bound to the wrong project — the exact isolation invariant this
// type of token exists to enforce.
type CreateAPITokenRequest struct {
	ProjectID uuid.UUID
	UserID    uuid.UUID
	Label     string
}

// CreateAPIToken mints a bearer token bound to exactly one project. The
// token value is returned once and never stored; nothing in this package
// ever holds it again. Nothing in this package ever updates project_id
// once a token is created — a token's project binding is fixed at
// creation and there is no query that could change it.
func (s *Service) CreateAPIToken(ctx context.Context, req CreateAPITokenRequest) (string, APITokenSummary, error) {
	label := strings.TrimSpace(req.Label)
	if n := utf8.RuneCountInString(label); n < minLabelRunes || n > maxLabelRunes {
		return "", APITokenSummary{}, fmt.Errorf("%w: label must be between %d and %d characters", ErrTokenRequestInvalid, minLabelRunes, maxLabelRunes)
	}

	body, err := newTokenBody()
	if err != nil {
		return "", APITokenSummary{}, err
	}
	token := TokenPrefix + body
	sum := sha256.Sum256([]byte(token))
	hint := body
	if n := utf8.RuneCountInString(hint); n > tokenHintRunes {
		hint = string([]rune(hint)[:tokenHintRunes])
	}

	row, err := s.q.CreateAPIToken(ctx, dbq.CreateAPITokenParams{
		TokenHash: sum[:],
		ProjectID: req.ProjectID,
		UserID:    req.UserID,
		Label:     label,
		TokenHint: hint,
	})
	if err != nil {
		if merr := mapAPITokenInsertError(err); merr != nil {
			return "", APITokenSummary{}, merr
		}
		return "", APITokenSummary{}, fmt.Errorf("create api token: %w", err)
	}
	return token, apiTokenSummaryFrom(row), nil
}

// mapAPITokenInsertError narrows a foreign-key violation from
// CreateAPIToken's insert to ErrTokenRequestInvalid, the same way
// projects.mapMembershipInsertError narrows one on memberships: checked
// by constraint name, not just SQLSTATE 23503, so a future unrelated
// foreign key on this table cannot be misreported as "bad request".
// Returns nil when err is not a foreign-key violation on either
// constraint, so the caller falls through to its own generic wrap.
func mapAPITokenInsertError(err error) error {
	var pgErr *pgconn.PgError
	if !errors.As(err, &pgErr) || pgErr.Code != "23503" {
		return nil
	}
	switch pgErr.ConstraintName {
	case "api_tokens_project_id_fkey", "api_tokens_user_id_fkey":
		return fmt.Errorf("%w: project or user does not exist", ErrTokenRequestInvalid)
	default:
		return nil
	}
}

// ResolveAPIToken maps a bearer value to its live token and records use.
// "Live" means not revoked; GetLiveAPIToken's own WHERE clause is the only
// thing that decides that, so there is exactly one place that answers
// "is this token still good".
//
// ResolveAPIToken answers authentication, not authorization: it proves
// the caller holds a value that hashes to a live row, and the ProjectID
// on the row it returns names what that token is bound to. It does not
// re-check whether the token's creator is still a member of that
// project — a caller that treats ProjectID as permission to act on that
// project must satisfy itself the binding is still one the product
// wants to honour. Today that guarantee comes from
// projects.RemoveMember revoking a departing member's tokens for that
// project in the same transaction as the membership deletion (see its
// own doc comment); nothing in this method re-derives that from
// memberships on every call, so a caller relying on ProjectID for
// authorization is trusting that revocation path, not re-verifying it.
func (s *Service) ResolveAPIToken(ctx context.Context, token string) (APITokenSummary, error) {
	if !verifyTokenChecksum(token) {
		// A mistyped or truncated paste never matches its own checksum,
		// so this is rejected without a database round trip at all — the
		// same reason the checksum exists in the first place (see
		// newTokenBody's doc comment).
		return APITokenSummary{}, ErrTokenInvalid
	}

	sum := sha256.Sum256([]byte(token))
	row, err := s.q.GetLiveAPIToken(ctx, sum[:])
	if err != nil {
		if errors.Is(err, pgx.ErrNoRows) {
			return APITokenSummary{}, ErrTokenInvalid
		}
		return APITokenSummary{}, fmt.Errorf("lookup api token: %w", err)
	}

	// Pre-checked in Go before ever issuing TouchAPIToken: GetLiveAPIToken
	// already returned last_used_at in row, so whether the write is even
	// worth attempting is known without a second statement. This is an
	// optimisation only — TouchAPIToken's own WHERE clause (identity.sql)
	// is what actually makes the write safe under two concurrent
	// resolves of the same token; it is left exactly as it was and is
	// still evaluated by Postgres regardless of this check.
	if !row.LastUsedAt.Valid || time.Since(row.LastUsedAt.Time) >= touchThrottle {
		if err := s.q.TouchAPIToken(ctx, row.ID); err != nil {
			// last_used_at is an audit convenience, not part of the
			// authentication decision: a statement timeout, a lock wait,
			// a read-only replica, or a cancelled context here must not
			// turn an otherwise-valid, already-authenticated token into a
			// hard failure on the hottest path in the product.
			slog.ErrorContext(ctx, "touch api token failed; continuing with the resolved token",
				"token_id", row.ID, "error", err)
		}
	}
	return apiTokenSummaryFrom(row), nil
}

// RevokeAPITokenRequest is the input to RevokeAPIToken. It exists for the
// same reason CreateAPITokenRequest above does: ProjectID and TokenID are
// both uuid.UUID.
type RevokeAPITokenRequest struct {
	ProjectID uuid.UUID
	TokenID   uuid.UUID
}

// RevokeAPIToken revokes a token, scoped to the project that owns it.
// Revoking an unknown token id, an already-revoked one, or one that
// belongs to a different project is not an error: the caller's goal (no
// live token under this id in this project) is already satisfied, the
// same convention RevokeSession and RevokeInvite already establish. The
// project scope in the query's own WHERE clause is what makes this safe
// to call with a project id taken from the caller's own request path
// without a separate ownership check: a caller can never revoke a token
// it does not administer, and never learns whether the id it named
// belongs to some other project instead.
func (s *Service) RevokeAPIToken(ctx context.Context, req RevokeAPITokenRequest) error {
	if err := s.q.RevokeAPIToken(ctx, dbq.RevokeAPITokenParams{ID: req.TokenID, ProjectID: req.ProjectID}); err != nil {
		return fmt.Errorf("revoke api token: %w", err)
	}
	return nil
}

// ListAPITokens lists every token of one project, newest first, revoked
// ones included — this is the audit surface an operator uses to notice a
// token that should have been revoked (an unrecognised label, a
// last-used time that doesn't match the agent it's supposed to be, one
// still live long after the work it was minted for finished) and to see
// what happened to one that already was. It never returns a token's
// value or hash. As this list grows, a filter (live-only by default) or
// a periodic prune of long-revoked rows will likely be wanted; neither
// exists yet.
func (s *Service) ListAPITokens(ctx context.Context, projectID uuid.UUID) ([]APITokenSummary, error) {
	rows, err := s.q.ListAPITokens(ctx, projectID)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, fmt.Errorf("list api tokens: %w", err)
	}
	out := make([]APITokenSummary, len(rows))
	for i, row := range rows {
		out[i] = APITokenSummary{
			ID:              row.ID,
			ProjectID:       row.ProjectID,
			UserID:          row.UserID,
			Label:           row.Label,
			TokenHint:       row.TokenHint,
			UserDisplayName: row.UserDisplayName,
			CreatedAt:       row.CreatedAt.Time,
		}
		if row.LastUsedAt.Valid {
			lastUsed := row.LastUsedAt.Time
			out[i].LastUsedAt = &lastUsed
		}
		if row.RevokedAt.Valid {
			revoked := row.RevokedAt.Time
			out[i].RevokedAt = &revoked
		}
	}
	return out, nil
}

// newTokenBody generates the random, non-prefix portion of a bearer
// token: randomTokenBytes of entropy (the same shared helper
// sessions.go's IssueSession and invites.go's CreateInvite use),
// base64url-encoded, followed by a tokenChecksumRunes-long base62 CRC32
// of that encoded string.
//
// The checksum is not a security control — CRC32 is not remotely
// collision-resistant against a determined attacker, and none of this
// package's authorization guarantees rest on it. What it buys is two
// things that only matter for a value with no attacker in the loop: a
// bare base64 body carries no distinctive structure, so a secret
// scanner's regex for "mst_" tokens either overmatches on unrelated
// base64 or has to fall back to entropy heuristics; appending a
// checksum a scanner can verify collapses that false-positive rate
// towards zero. And a human who mistypes or truncates a paste gets
// ErrTokenInvalid straight out of verifyTokenChecksum, with no database
// round trip, instead of a lookup miss indistinguishable from "this
// value was never valid at all".
func newTokenBody() (string, error) {
	raw, err := randomToken()
	if err != nil {
		return "", err
	}
	return raw + tokenChecksum(raw), nil
}

// verifyTokenChecksum reports whether token has TokenPrefix and a body
// ending in a checksum that matches the body's own random portion. It is
// the first thing ResolveAPIToken checks, before any hashing or
// database access.
func verifyTokenChecksum(token string) bool {
	body, ok := strings.CutPrefix(token, TokenPrefix)
	if !ok || len(body) <= tokenChecksumRunes {
		return false
	}
	random := body[:len(body)-tokenChecksumRunes]
	checksum := body[len(body)-tokenChecksumRunes:]
	return tokenChecksum(random) == checksum
}

// tokenChecksum returns the tokenChecksumRunes-long base62 encoding of
// random's CRC32-IEEE checksum.
func tokenChecksum(random string) string {
	return base62Encode(crc32.ChecksumIEEE([]byte(random)), tokenChecksumRunes)
}

// base62EncodeAlphabet is ordered digits-then-uppercase-then-lowercase so
// two encodings of nearby inputs do not casually resemble each other
// (not a security property — see newTokenBody — just a readability
// choice for the humans this hint and checksum exist for).
const base62EncodeAlphabet = "0123456789ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZabcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz"

// base62Encode encodes n as a fixed-width, zero-left-padded base62
// string of exactly width characters. width is always large enough to
// hold any uint32 for this file's one caller (tokenChecksum, with
// width == tokenChecksumRunes == 6; 62^6 > 2^32), so this never
// truncates significant digits.
func base62Encode(n uint32, width int) string {
	buf := make([]byte, width)
	for i := width - 1; i >= 0; i-- {
		buf[i] = base62EncodeAlphabet[n%62]
		n /= 62
	}
	return string(buf)
}
```

**Corrections made during implementation, round 1** (this task's own
first review pass — the original snippets returned `dbq.ApiToken` and
`dbq.ListAPITokensRow` directly, took two adjacent `uuid.UUID` parameters
positionally on both mutating methods, and validated nothing about the
label). A second round follows below it, from a quality review of the
committed round-1 code; where the two disagree, the round-2 entry is
what the final code above actually does — most visibly, round 1's
`APIToken` type is `APITokenSummary` by the time round 2 is done with it
(Correction 13), and round 1's Correction 6 (below) describes a plan
that round 2's Correction 9 overrides.

1. `CreateAPIToken`, `ResolveAPIToken` and `ListAPITokens` now return a
   domain type, never `dbq.ApiToken` or `dbq.ListAPITokensRow` — the
   same rule `identity.User` (Task 5) and `InviteSummary` (Task 7)
   already follow, for the same reason: `dbq.ApiToken` carries `TokenHash`,
   and a raw sqlc struct crossing the package boundary is one careless
   `writeJSON` away from serving a hash of the bearer credential back to
   whoever asked. `ListAPITokens`'s query already projected away
   `token_hash` at the SQL level (the plan's own snippet did this
   correctly), so this correction is about the Go boundary, not the query.
   (Round 1 named this type `APIToken`; round 2 renamed it — see
   Correction 13.)
2. `CreateAPIToken` now takes a `CreateAPITokenRequest{ProjectID, UserID,
   Label}` struct instead of `(projectID, userID uuid.UUID, label string)`,
   and `RevokeAPIToken` takes a `RevokeAPITokenRequest{ProjectID,
   TokenID}` instead of `(projectID, tokenID uuid.UUID)`. Both original
   signatures put two adjacent `uuid.UUID` parameters next to each other —
   exactly the shape `CreateUserRequest` (Task 5, Correction 11) exists to
   rule out, and worse here than there: a transposed `projectID`/`userID`
   at a `CreateAPIToken` call site would still compile and would silently
   mint a token bound to the wrong project — a direct hole in the one
   invariant this task exists to enforce, not merely a corrupted display
   name. `RevokeAPITokenRequest` is not the security-critical half of this
   correction (a transposed id there only makes the revoke a no-op, since
   `RevokeAPIToken`'s WHERE clause already requires both to match — see
   correction 4), but it exists anyway for consistency with `CreateAPIToken`
   and because "some positional pairs stay, some don't" is a worse rule
   than "none do" for the type most directly responsible for the token/
   project binding.
   This ripples into every later task's plan snippets that call
   `CreateAPIToken` or `RevokeAPIToken` positionally (Tasks 10, 12, 13);
   those snippets have been updated in place to construct the request
   structs instead, each with a one-line note pointing back here. Anyone
   implementing those tasks from a stale copy of this plan should replace
   `ids.CreateAPIToken(ctx, projectID, userID, label)` with
   `ids.CreateAPIToken(ctx, identity.CreateAPITokenRequest{ProjectID:
   projectID, UserID: userID, Label: label})`, and the equivalent for
   `RevokeAPIToken`.
3. The label is now trimmed and bounded to 1-200 runes
   (`ErrTokenRequestInvalid` on violation), matching the bound
   `projects.validateName` applies to a project's name. The original
   snippet stored whatever string a caller sent with no check at all — an
   empty label would have made `ListAPITokens` useless as the "which
   token is this" surface it exists to be (see correction 5), and an
   unbounded one is the same unbounded-text-column exposure Task 5 and
   Task 8 both already closed for every other user-supplied string in this
   codebase.
4. `RevokeAPIToken`'s query gained an explicit `AND revoked_at IS NULL` to
   its `WHERE` clause. It was not required for correctness — an
   already-revoked row updated again just sets `revoked_at` to a later
   `now()`, which is harmless — but it was added to make the no-op
   convention (see the query's own comment) hold by construction rather
   than by coincidence, and to keep `revoked_at` reporting the moment a
   token was actually revoked, not the moment someone last redundantly
   tried to revoke an already-dead one.
5. `ResolveAPIToken`'s doc comment now says explicitly that a revoked
   token and a never-existed one are indistinguishable by construction,
   the same way `ErrNoSession`'s comment (Task 6) already does for
   sessions. This was checked deliberately, not assumed: `GetLiveAPIToken`
   filters on `revoked_at IS NULL` in the query itself, so there is no
   later branch that could leak the distinction even if a future change
   wanted to add one without noticing.
6. (Superseded — see Round 2 Correction 9.) This correction went through
   three shapes across two reviews, in order: first, revoking a removed
   member's tokens was left unbuilt with a note that `ListAPITokens` was
   the mitigation; a first review correctly rejected that (a review
   surface an admin has to remember to check is not a safeguard) and
   routed the fix to Task 12's membership-management handler, on the
   reasoning that Task 12 was the first place `RemoveMember` gets a
   caller at all. A second review rejected *that* too, and rightly: Task
   10's auth middleware reads a resolved token's `ProjectID` as the
   caller's scope starting the moment Task 10 exists, several tasks
   before Task 12 would have closed this — so routing the fix to Task 12
   left every game in between with a live hole for any member who was
   ever removed. The fix now lives in this task, inside
   `projects.RemoveMember` itself, done before this task is considered
   finished rather than promised to a later one. See Round 2 Correction 9
   for the final shape.
7. Not changed, and confirmed correct on inspection: `api_tokens.user_id`
   and `api_tokens.project_id` are both `NOT NULL ... ON DELETE CASCADE`
   (migration 0001, already applied as of Task 5). Deleting the user who
   created a token, or deleting the project it belongs to, hard-deletes
   the token row rather than leaving an orphaned or dangling one — there
   is no path by which a live token can end up pointing at a project or a
   user that no longer exists. This does mean the row (and with it,
   `created_at`/`last_used_at` history) disappears rather than being kept
   for audit purposes the way a revoked-but-retained row would be; that
   trade-off was made once already, in the schema this task inherited
   rather than wrote, and revisiting it (e.g. a nullable `user_id` with
   `ON DELETE SET NULL`, mirroring `invites.created_by`) is a migration
   change out of this task's scope.
8. Test file grew from the plan's two tests to eight: the two original
   tests plus coverage for unique token generation, label validation,
   project-scoped revocation refusing to touch another project's token
   (`TestRevokeAPITokenIsScopedToItsOwnProject`), `ListAPITokens` scoping
   to one project and never returning a hash-shaped field, revoked-
   vs-unknown indistinguishability
   (`TestRevokedTokenIsIndistinguishableFromUnknown`), and — added in a
   second pass, after a review correctly flagged that nothing exercised
   it — the `last_used_at` throttle
   (`TestResolveAPITokenThrottlesLastUsedAtWrites`). That gap mattered
   specifically because it was the one behaviour in this file with a time
   dependency: without a test reading the column's actual value before
   and after a backdated resolve, a later refactor could drop
   `TouchAPIToken`'s five-minute condition and go back to writing on
   every authenticated request, and the suite would not have noticed. The
   test backdates `last_used_at` directly with SQL (the same technique
   `TestExpiredSessionIsRejectedAndPruned`, Task 6, uses for `expires_at`)
   rather than sleeping past the window, so it is deterministic and fast.
   Every other behaviour this task added is covered; nothing in
   `tokens.go` is exercised only by inspection.

**Round 2 corrections** (a quality review of the committed round-1 code,
after the round-1 fixes above had already landed):

9. **Membership removal now revokes that member's tokens for that game,
   inside this task, not deferred to Task 12.** This is the fix Round 1
   Correction 6 (above) describes arriving at after two false starts. The
   final shape: `internal/db/queries/projects.sql` gained
   `RevokeAPITokensForMember` (`UPDATE api_tokens SET revoked_at = now()
   WHERE user_id = $1 AND project_id = $2 AND revoked_at IS NULL`), and
   `projects.RemoveMember` (Task 8) now calls it inside its own `withTx`,
   after `DeleteMembership` succeeds — see Step 3 above for both. This
   was rejected once for living in `identity` (wrong: `api_tokens.user_id`
   is provenance, not a live authorization link, and `identity` gaining a
   dependency on `projects` — or the reverse — was never on the table)
   and once for waiting on Task 12 (wrong: Task 10's auth middleware
   trusts a resolved token's `ProjectID` as the caller's scope from the
   moment Task 10 exists, several tasks before Task 12 would have shipped
   the fix, so every game in between would have had a live hole for any
   removed member's still-working token). Landing it here, as plain SQL
   over `api_tokens` reached through the `dbq.Queries` handle both
   packages already share — the same handle `RemoveMember` already uses
   to call `UpsertMembership`, defined in `identity.sql`, the other
   direction — closes it before this task is considered done instead of
   promising a later one will. `ResolveAPIToken`'s doc comment now says
   explicitly that it answers *authentication*, not *authorization*: the
   `ProjectID` it returns names the token's binding, and a caller
   treating that as permission is trusting this revocation path, not
   re-deriving membership on every call (the review's rejected
   alternative — joining `memberships` inside `GetLiveAPIToken` — was
   considered and set aside for putting a second table on the hottest
   path in the product and making token validity implicit; explicit
   revocation keeps `revoked_at` recording *when* and *why*, and
   `ListAPITokens` keeps telling the truth about what happened). Tests:
   `TestRemoveMemberRevokesTheirTokensInThatProject` and
   `TestRemoveMemberLeavesTheirTokensInOtherProjectsAlone`
   (`internal/projects/projects_test.go`), the second pinning the exact
   blast radius — that project only, never every game the member
   belongs to.
10. **The hot path no longer pays two round trips when it only needs
    one.** `GetLiveAPIToken` already does `SELECT *`, so `row.LastUsedAt`
    is in hand before `ResolveAPIToken` decides anything; the round-1
    code issued `TouchAPIToken` unconditionally regardless, meaning
    essentially every authenticated agent request paid a second pool
    checkout and a second statement for an `UPDATE` the database was
    guaranteed to match zero rows for. `ResolveAPIToken` now checks
    `!row.LastUsedAt.Valid || time.Since(row.LastUsedAt.Time) >=
    touchThrottle` in Go before issuing the statement at all.
    `TouchAPIToken`'s own SQL `WHERE` clause is unchanged — the Go check
    is an optimisation on top of it, not a replacement: only the SQL
    predicate, evaluated by Postgres inside the UPDATE itself, actually
    serializes two concurrent resolves of the same token, which a
    single-threaded Go time comparison cannot guarantee on its own. The
    five-minute window now has one authoritative home,
    `touchThrottle = 5 * time.Minute` in `tokens.go`, with the SQL
    comment on `TouchAPIToken` pointing at it explicitly ("change one,
    change both") — round 1 left the five minutes as a bare literal in
    the query with nothing on the Go side to drift against, which is
    exactly the kind of two-copies-of-one-policy Task 6's `IssueSession`
    doc comment already argued against for session expiry.
    Separately: a failed `TouchAPIToken` (a statement timeout, a lock
    wait, a read-only replica, a cancelled context) no longer fails
    `ResolveAPIToken`. `last_used_at` is an audit convenience, not part
    of the authentication decision, so a bookkeeping write that can't
    complete now logs (`slog.ErrorContext`, matching the pattern
    `users.go`'s rehash-on-login failure already uses) and returns the
    resolved token — turning a valid, already-authenticated credential
    into a hard 401 over a write that exists purely so an operator can
    see when a token was last used would have been a self-inflicted
    outage on the hottest path in the product. `APITokenSummary`'s own
    doc comment now notes that the `LastUsedAt` it returns can be up to
    one `touchThrottle` interval stale relative to what the database
    holds immediately afterward, for the same reason: it reflects the
    row as read at the start of the call, before that call's own
    (possibly skipped, possibly failed-and-logged) touch.
11. **`ListAPITokens` grew a `token_hint` column, a join to `users`, and
    an explicit tiebreak, and `ListOutstandingInvites` got the same
    tiebreak fix.** An operator who finds a leaked value like
    "mst_xK9qP2af…" in a CI log or a committed `.env` had no way to map
    it back to a row — the one job this listing exists for. `api_tokens`
    now carries `token_hint text NOT NULL`
    (`internal/db/migrations/0003_api_token_hint.sql`, a new migration —
    this codebase does not edit an applied one, migration 0001 included),
    populated by `CreateAPIToken` from the first `tokenHintRunes` (8)
    characters of the token's random body — never a prefix of the hash,
    and never enough of the body to reconstruct the token or meaningfully
    narrow a brute-force search of the rest. `ListAPITokens`'s query now
    joins `users` so the listing shows who minted a token instead of a
    raw id, and orders by `created_at DESC, id DESC` instead of
    `created_at DESC` alone — the same missing-tiebreak class of bug
    `ListProjectsForUser` and `ListMembers` (Task 8) already fixed for
    their own listings, which two tokens minted in the same transaction
    would have hit immediately. `ListOutstandingInvites` in the same
    query file shared the identical drift (`ORDER BY created_at DESC`,
    no tiebreak) and is fixed the same way while this file was already
    open for it. `ListAPITokens` still returns revoked rows unfiltered —
    correct for now, since this is the audit trail for what happened to
    a token, not only a list of what is live — but its doc comment now
    flags that a live-only filter or a periodic prune will likely be
    wanted once this list is long enough that scrolling past every
    revoked row to find a live one is the normal case, not an edge one.
12. **Every minted token now carries a verifiable checksum in its body,**
    appended by `newTokenBody` and checked by `verifyTokenChecksum`
    before `ResolveAPIToken` ever hashes the value or queries the
    database. The checksum is a `tokenChecksumRunes`-long (6) base62
    encoding of the `crc32.ChecksumIEEE` of the token's random body —
    not a security control (CRC32 has no collision resistance against a
    deliberate attacker, and no authorization guarantee in this package
    rests on it), but it earns two things a bare base64 body cannot: a
    secret-scanner's regex for `mst_`-prefixed tokens can verify the
    checksum and reject unrelated base64 that happens to start the same
    way, pushing the false-positive rate that makes scanners get
    disabled near zero; and a human who mistypes or truncates a paste
    gets `ErrTokenInvalid` immediately, with no database round trip,
    instead of a lookup miss indistinguishable from "this value was
    never valid at all". `TokenPrefix`'s doc comment now says explicitly
    that the prefix is inside the value `CreateAPIToken` hashes (the
    hash covers `TokenPrefix + body`, not `body` alone, unchanged from
    round 1), so changing `TokenPrefix` invalidates every token already
    issued — worth stating now, while no token has ever existed in
    production, rather than leaving it to be rediscovered the first time
    someone considers changing it. `TestResolveAPITokenRejectsTamperedTokenWithoutTouchingTheDatabase`
    covers this.
13. **Minor renames and fixes**, all requested directly by the round-2
    review: the domain type is `APITokenSummary`, not `APIToken` —
    matching `InviteSummary`'s naming (Task 7) so the type name itself
    signals "not the credential", the same distinction `InviteSummary`
    already draws for invites. The `clear` local variable and parameter
    name (in `tokens.go` and, for consistency, in every test that used
    it) is now `token` — `clear` shadows the Go 1.21+ builtin `clear()`
    and read oddly next to `sessions.go`'s and `invites.go`'s own
    `token` locals for the identical value. `CreateAPIToken` now maps a
    `23503` foreign-key violation on `api_tokens_project_id_fkey` or
    `api_tokens_user_id_fkey` to `ErrTokenRequestInvalid` via
    `mapAPITokenInsertError`, the same constraint-name-checked pattern
    `projects.mapMembershipInsertError` already establishes — round 1
    let a bad project or user id surface as a generic wrapped error
    (a 500 at the HTTP layer once Task 12 exists), where every sibling
    package already turns the equivalent violation into a typed 4xx.
    The `ListAPITokens` re-hydration round 1 did through a synthetic
    `dbq.ApiToken{...}` (built field-by-field from `ListAPITokensRow` just
    to hand it back to the same `apiTokenFrom` helper `CreateAPIToken`
    and `ResolveAPIToken` use) is gone rather than commented: now that
    the join adds `UserDisplayName` and the row shape has genuinely
    diverged from `dbq.ApiToken`, `ListAPITokens` builds `APITokenSummary`
    directly from its own row instead of faking up a row shape that only
    coincidentally matched before this round's join.
14. Test file grew from round 1's eight tests to twelve in
    `internal/identity/tokens_test.go`
    (`TestResolveAPITokenReturnsTheCorrectProjectAmongMany`,
    `TestRevokeUnknownAPITokenIsANoOp`,
    `TestRevokeAlreadyRevokedAPITokenIsANoOp`, and
    `TestResolveAPITokenRejectsTamperedTokenWithoutTouchingTheDatabase`
    added this round), plus two new tests in
    `internal/projects/projects_test.go`
    (`TestRemoveMemberRevokesTheirTokensInThatProject`,
    `TestRemoveMemberLeavesTheirTokensInOtherProjectsAlone` — see
    Correction 9). The most important addition is
    `TestResolveAPITokenReturnsTheCorrectProjectAmongMany`: the review
    correctly pointed out that
    `TestAPITokenResolvesToItsProject` runs against a database holding
    exactly one project, so its assertion that a resolved token names
    "the" project passes even if project scoping were silently dropped
    from `ResolveAPIToken` entirely — the new test creates two games and
    a token in each and checks that each resolves to its own binding,
    not the other's, which is what this package's isolation invariant is
    actually about. The two explicit no-op tests
    (`TestRevokeUnknownAPITokenIsANoOp`,
    `TestRevokeAlreadyRevokedAPITokenIsANoOp`) close the gap the review
    also found: both behaviours were documented in `RevokeAPIToken`'s doc
    comment and in the query's own comment since round 1, and neither had
    a test of its own until now.

### Task 10: Authentication middleware and the caller context

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/web/auth.go`
- Modify: `internal/web/server.go`
- Test: `internal/web/auth_test.go`

**Note (from Task 9):** `identity.CreateAPIToken` and `RevokeAPIToken`
take `CreateAPITokenRequest`/`RevokeAPITokenRequest` structs, not
positional `uuid.UUID` pairs (Round 1 Correction 2); the snippets below
already reflect that. `ResolveAPIToken` returns `identity.APITokenSummary`
(renamed from `APIToken` in Round 2, Correction 13), whose field names
(`ID`, `ProjectID`, `UserID`) match what the plan's original
`dbq.ApiToken`-based snippets already assumed, so nothing else here
changed shape. Also from Task 9 (Round 2, Correction 9): `ResolveAPIToken`
answers authentication only — the `ProjectID` this middleware puts on
`Caller` is trustworthy as a scope specifically because
`projects.RemoveMember` revokes a departing member's tokens for that
project itself; this middleware does not, and must not, re-derive
membership on every request.

**Note (from Task 6's round-2 review, Correction 14):** this task, or
whichever task first gives the process a background-job lifecycle, should
wire a ticker that calls `identity.Service.PruneExpiredSessions` on some
interval (hourly is plenty) and logs how many rows it deleted. Task 6
deliberately left `PruneExpiredSessions` unwired — an unpruned session row
is disk bloat, not a security exposure — so nothing currently calls it.

`internal/web/auth.go`:

```go
package web

import (
	"context"
	"errors"
	"net/http"
	"strings"

	"github.com/google/uuid"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/identity"
)

// SessionCookie is the name of the browser session cookie.
const SessionCookie = "maestro_session"

// Caller is the authenticated principal of a request. A caller authenticated
// by an API token carries the single project that token is bound to; a caller
// authenticated by a session cookie carries none, and resolves the project
// from the URL instead.
type Caller struct {
	UserID    uuid.UUID
	IsAdmin   bool
	TokenID   *uuid.UUID
	ProjectID *uuid.UUID
}

type callerKey struct{}

// CallerFrom returns the authenticated caller of a request, if any.
func CallerFrom(ctx context.Context) (Caller, bool) {
	c, ok := ctx.Value(callerKey{}).(Caller)
	return c, ok
}

// authenticate resolves a bearer token or a session cookie into a Caller and
// puts it in the request context. It never fails the request: handlers decide
// whether anonymous access is acceptable.
func (s *Server) authenticate(next http.Handler) http.Handler {
	return http.HandlerFunc(func(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {
		ctx := r.Context()

		if header := r.Header.Get("Authorization"); strings.HasPrefix(header, "Bearer ") {
			token := strings.TrimSpace(strings.TrimPrefix(header, "Bearer "))
			row, err := s.opts.Identity.ResolveAPIToken(ctx, token)
			if err == nil {
				projectID := row.ProjectID
				user, err := s.opts.Identity.UserByID(ctx, row.UserID)
				if err == nil {
					ctx = context.WithValue(ctx, callerKey{}, Caller{
						UserID:    row.UserID,
						IsAdmin:   user.IsAdmin,
						TokenID:   &row.ID,
						ProjectID: &projectID,
					})
				}
			} else if !errors.Is(err, identity.ErrTokenInvalid) {
				writeError(w, http.StatusInternalServerError, "internal_error", "could not verify the token")
				return
			}
			next.ServeHTTP(w, r.WithContext(ctx))
			return
		}

		if cookie, err := r.Cookie(SessionCookie); err == nil {
			// UserForSession also returns the session's own expiry
			// (Task 6, Correction 12), for a sliding-renewal policy that
			// would call identity.ExtendSession here once the remaining
			// lifetime drops below some threshold. Not implemented yet:
			// today's sessions are absolute, so the expiry is discarded.
			user, _, err := s.opts.Identity.UserForSession(ctx, cookie.Value)
			if err == nil {
				ctx = context.WithValue(ctx, callerKey{}, Caller{UserID: user.ID, IsAdmin: user.IsAdmin})
			}
		}

		next.ServeHTTP(w, r.WithContext(ctx))
	})
}

// requireCaller wraps a handler so anonymous requests get a 401.
func requireCaller(h func(http.ResponseWriter, *http.Request, Caller)) http.HandlerFunc {
	return func(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {
		caller, ok := CallerFrom(r.Context())
		if !ok {
			writeError(w, http.StatusUnauthorized, "unauthorized", "authentication required")
			return
		}
		h(w, r, caller)
	}
}

func writeError(w http.ResponseWriter, status int, code, message string) {
	writeJSON(w, status, map[string]string{"error": code, "message": message})
}
```

```go
// UserByID loads one user.
func (s *Service) UserByID(ctx context.Context, id uuid.UUID) (User, error) {
	dbUser, err := s.q.GetUserByID(ctx, id)
	if err != nil {
		return User{}, fmt.Errorf("lookup user: %w", err)
	}
	return userFrom(dbUser), nil
}
```

`users.go` already imports `"github.com/google/uuid"` as of Task 5 (it is
needed there for `identity.User.ID`), so this needs no new import.

- [ ] **Step 5: Extend the server to carry services and the `/api/me` route**

Replace `Options` and `NewServer` in `internal/web/server.go`:

```go
// Options carries everything the server needs from the outside.
type Options struct {
	Version  string
	Config   config.Config
	Identity *identity.Service
	Projects *projects.Service
}

// NewServer builds the routing tree.
func NewServer(opts Options) *Server {
	s := &Server{mux: http.NewServeMux(), opts: opts}
	s.mux.HandleFunc("GET /healthz", s.handleHealthz)
	s.mux.HandleFunc("GET /version", s.handleVersion)
	s.mux.Handle("GET /api/me", requireCaller(s.handleMe))
	return s
}

// ServeHTTP runs every request through authentication first.
func (s *Server) ServeHTTP(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {
	s.authenticate(s.mux).ServeHTTP(w, r)
}

func (s *Server) handleMe(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request, caller Caller) {
	payload := map[string]any{
		"user_id":  caller.UserID,
		"is_admin": caller.IsAdmin,
	}
	if caller.ProjectID != nil {
		payload["project_id"] = *caller.ProjectID
	}
	writeJSON(w, http.StatusOK, payload)
}
```

Add the `config`, `identity` and `projects` imports.

### Task 11: Login, logout, registration and invite redemption over REST

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/web/api_auth.go`
- Modify: `internal/web/server.go`
- Test: `internal/web/api_auth_test.go`

`handleRegister` calls `identity.CreateUser` with a `CreateUserRequest`
struct now, not four positional arguments (Task 5, Correction 11), and
`writeRegistrationError` gains three more cases — `ErrEmailInvalid`,
`ErrDisplayNameInvalid`, `ErrPasswordInvalid` — mapped to 422, since Task 5
added those typed errors specifically so this HTTP boundary could
distinguish "malformed input" from the other 400/403/409 cases already
handled. Both are reflected in the snippet below.

Task 6's round-2 review (Corrections 8-11) changes this task's shape in
four ways, all reflected in the snippet below:

- `identity.Limiter.Allow` is gone; `handleLogin` calls `Allowed` before
  attempting the login and `Record` only if `Authenticate` fails. Nothing
  calls `Reset` any more — a successful login was never charged in the
  first place, so there is nothing to refund.
- `handleLogin` normalizes `req.Email` (lower-case, trim) before using it
  as the limiter key, matching `Authenticate`'s own normalization, so
  three different capitalizations of the same address share one budget
  instead of three.
- `handleRegister`'s invite-redemption limiter keys on the request's
  client IP (`clientIP(r)`, added below) instead of `req.InviteToken` —
  keying on the secret being guessed let every guess mint a fresh,
  unlimited budget.
- `identity.SessionTTL` no longer exists; `IssueSession` returns
  `(token string, expiresAt time.Time, err error)`, and
  `setSessionCookie` takes `expiresAt` as a parameter instead of
  recomputing `time.Now().Add(identity.SessionTTL)` itself.

```go
package web_test

import (
	"context"
	"net/http"
	"net/http/httptest"
	"strings"
	"testing"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/web"
)

func TestLoginSetsSessionCookieAndLogoutClearsIt(t *testing.T) {
	srv, ids, _ := newTestServer(t)
	ctx := context.Background()
	if _, err := ids.CreateUser(ctx, identity.CreateUserRequest{Email: "designer@studio.com", DisplayName: "Designer", Password: "password12345"}); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("CreateUser: %v", err)
	}

	body := strings.NewReader(`{"email":"designer@studio.com","password":"password12345"}`)
	req := httptest.NewRequest(http.MethodPost, "/api/auth/login", body)
	req.Header.Set("Content-Type", "application/json")
	rec := httptest.NewRecorder()
	srv.ServeHTTP(rec, req)

	if rec.Code != http.StatusOK {
		t.Fatalf("login status = %d, want 200; body = %s", rec.Code, rec.Body.String())
	}
	cookies := rec.Result().Cookies()
	var session *http.Cookie
	for _, c := range cookies {
		if c.Name == web.SessionCookie {
			session = c
		}
	}
	if session == nil {
		t.Fatal("login did not set a session cookie")
	}
	if !session.HttpOnly {
		t.Fatal("the session cookie must be HttpOnly")
	}
	if session.SameSite != http.SameSiteLaxMode {
		t.Fatal("the session cookie must be SameSite=Lax")
	}

	meReq := httptest.NewRequest(http.MethodGet, "/api/me", nil)
	meReq.AddCookie(session)
	meRec := httptest.NewRecorder()
	srv.ServeHTTP(meRec, meReq)
	if meRec.Code != http.StatusOK {
		t.Fatalf("/api/me with the session cookie = %d, want 200", meRec.Code)
	}

	outReq := httptest.NewRequest(http.MethodPost, "/api/auth/logout", nil)
	outReq.AddCookie(session)
	outRec := httptest.NewRecorder()
	srv.ServeHTTP(outRec, outReq)
	if outRec.Code != http.StatusNoContent {
		t.Fatalf("logout status = %d, want 204", outRec.Code)
	}

	afterReq := httptest.NewRequest(http.MethodGet, "/api/me", nil)
	afterReq.AddCookie(session)
	afterRec := httptest.NewRecorder()
	srv.ServeHTTP(afterRec, afterReq)
	if afterRec.Code != http.StatusUnauthorized {
		t.Fatalf("/api/me after logout = %d, want 401", afterRec.Code)
	}
}

	if _, err := ids.CreateUser(context.Background(), identity.CreateUserRequest{Email: "designer@studio.com", DisplayName: "Designer", Password: "password12345"}); err != nil {
```
